770 questions about players, teams and the game, each answered with data. Each carries its answer (supported, not supported, mixed) and how far the evidence goes: tested only on the data it was found in, held out, or replicated on later data. Method.

770 tests

Asked and answered 14

Does sending players to the offensive glass leave a team open in transition?

No, in the NBA it costs almost nothing. For every 5 points of offensive rebound rate a team adds, opponents score +0.002 points per possession after its misses (90% interval −0.007 to +0.011); the cost would have to reach +0.086 to cancel the extra rebounds. Net, each 5 points of offensive rebound rate gains +6.6 points per 100 misses (5.6 to 7.5).

664,302 missed field goals in 180 team-seasons of play-by-play, 2020-21 to 2025-26. Each team's offensive rebound rate is compared with what opponents score on the possessions after its misses, within each season; the same team compared with itself from year to year gives the same answer. The net counts the possession the opponent gets afterwards. Intervals: 90%, resampling franchises. Blind to: how many players actually crash (offensive rebound rate is an outcome, not a count), and the controls for big men and team quality are partial.

Supportedq-crash

Should a coach sit a starter who picks up two early fouls?

Usually not. After a second foul in the first quarter a starter loses 3.9 game minutes against his normal (90% interval 3.8 to 4.0), and leaving him in keeps 8.5 more minutes while raising his foul-out risk from 3.5% to 10.2%. Priced in wins, leaving him in is worth +0.33 points of win probability per decision (+0.28 to +0.38), about 0.22 wins per team per season.

124,505 foul-trouble events at four trouble points (second foul in the first quarter through fifth foul in the fourth). Each starter is compared with himself in his other starts that season, at the same second of the game with fewer fouls. A simple model of when fouls arrive gives the minutes kept and the foul-out risk; the win value multiplies those minutes by the rating gap between the starter and the teammates who replace him. Intervals: 90%, resampling players; the decision-model intervals cover sampling only. Blind to: matchups, what the coach knows, and minutes coaches give back later (scaled to those, the win value is about half).

Supportedq-two-foul

How surprising is it that LeBron never had a single 27-point, 7-rebound, 7-assist game?

Only mildly. A model built from his own 1,622 games expects 1.65 games at exactly 27/7/7 (90% interval 1.29 to 2.05), so getting none happens about 19% of the time. Across all 1,191 players with 400+ games, 25.5% never posted their own rounded career line (23.4% to 27.5%).

Regular-season games with minutes above zero, players with 400+ games and nearly complete box scores, so this covers the modern box-score era. The model (a Gaussian copula) keeps each player's own spread of points, rebounds and assists and joins them with his own correlations between them; treating the three as independent predicts too few exact lines. Lines are rounded career averages. Intervals: 90%, resampling games.

Not supportedq-line

Did EPM, a leading public player rating, change its model?

Yes. Its 2025-26 values were re-issued between 7 Oct 2026 and 20 Oct 2026; for players with 500+ minutes the order barely moved (rank correlation 0.914, 90% interval 0.896 to 0.928), but the spread is 14% wider (10% to 17%). Singular's comparisons with EPM still stand, because they scored the values EPM published at the time.

Our saved copy of the table from 7 Oct 2026 against a fresh pull on 20 Oct 2026, matched player by player. Intervals: 90%, resampling players. The old season table rests on one archived copy, and past-season tables are now behind a subscription.

Supportedq-epm

Is an isolation-heavy offense easier to shut down in the playoffs?

No sign of it. For the same player in the same season, isolation and pick-and-roll plays lose 0.019 points per possession less from the regular season to the playoffs than his other plays (90% interval +0.006 to +0.032). Teams that lean on those plays do no worse in playoff series than their regular-season ratings predict (−0.89 points per 100 possessions per 10 points of share, −2.33 to +0.56); the test rules out a penalty larger than about 13 points of series win probability.

NBA.com Synergy play types, 2015-16 to 2025-26: 695 player-seasons compare each player's isolation and pick-and-roll-handler plays with his other plays, adjusted for the better defenses teams meet in the playoffs. The series test was declared before any outcome was computed: 919 games in 165 series, with a play-by-play stand-in for style that extends it back to 1996-97. Intervals: 90%, resampling players (play types) and team-seasons (series). Blind to: Synergy credits a pick-and-roll to the handler only when he finishes it, so a trap that forces a pass shows up elsewhere; only good teams reach the playoffs.

Not supportedq-iso

Does Stop%, the share of possessions a defender ends, measure good defense?

It measures something stable, but it adds nothing to existing defensive ratings. It carries over from season to season at r = 0.88, yet it tracks defensive RAPM (a lineup-based defense rating) less well than the box-score rating DBPM (0.33 against 0.50). Added to DBPM, it changes next-season prediction error by −0.01% (90% interval −0.06% to +0.05%).

Player-seasons from 2003-04 to 2025-26; the correlations use 6,013 player-seasons with 1,000+ minutes. The out-of-sample test fits on 2003-13 and predicts next season's defensive RAPM in 2014-24. Intervals: 90%, resampling players. Blind to: shot deterrence, positional defense and switching, which box scores do not see.

Not supportedClean nullq-stop-pct

Does preseason tell you anything about how a player's season will go?

Yes: a little for returning players, a lot for rookies. Tested on seasons the model never saw, preseason box scores add 0.023 to the share of variation explained (R²) in a second- or third-year player's season rating (90% interval 0.012 to 0.036), and 0.137 for rookies over draft slot and age (0.089 to 0.181). Among the top tenth of preseason-flagged risers, 71% beat their forecast, against 52% of all returning players.

Player-seasons with 60+ preseason minutes and 500+ regular-season minutes, preseasons 2005-06 to 2025-26. Each season is forecast by a model fit only on earlier seasons (walk-forward), with and without the preseason box score; the season rating is BPM (Box Plus/Minus). Intervals: 90%, resampling players. Blind to: stars resting in preseason, opponent quality and effort, and players whose bad preseason cost them their role (the minutes filter drops them).

Supportedq-preseason

Are high-level college shooters failing to make it in the NBA?

Not as a group. Shooters from the 2018-23 draft classes played 330 more NBA minutes in their first three seasons than other picks, both measured against what their draft slot predicts (90% interval −23 to +669). The gap did not shrink in recent classes (+198, −316 to +693), though the players the posts name are a real cluster of misses: 5 of 8 played less than their slot predicts.

Drafted players with a final college season in Jeremias Engelmann's public pre-draft data, classes 2010-25. A shooter made 38%+ of his threes on 5+ attempts per 40 minutes. Expected minutes come from a draft-slot curve; players who never played count as zero. Intervals: 90%, resampling players. Blind to: minutes measure opportunity, not quality; the 2024 and 2025 classes have one or two seasons.

Not supportedq-college-shooters

Do unstable rotations hold young players back?

We found no sign of it. Rotation habits belong to the coach (with the same coach, stability carries over at r = 0.54, 90% interval 0.46 to 0.62), but for players 23 or under, one standard deviation more stability goes with −0.155 BPM of development (−0.260 to −0.059), the opposite of the claim. Donovan's six Chicago teams sat about average (−0.020 against the season mean, −0.049 to +0.007).

390 team-seasons from lineup stints, 2013-14 to 2025-26. The measure compares each rotation player's minute-by-minute on-court pattern across every pair of games: 1 means the same minutes every night, 0 means they look random. Counting coach's-decision benchings changes almost nothing. Stable seasons do go with better results beyond the roster (+0.68 points per 100 per standard deviation, +0.42 to +0.96), but nothing shows the coach causes it. Intervals: 90%, cluster bootstrap. This is observational: injured rosters make rotations unstable, and good young players earn fixed roles.

Not supportedq-rotations

When a player's scoring role shrinks, do his rebounding and defense grow?

Mostly no, in either direction. When roster changes move a player's usage by 5 points, his defensive rebounds change by +0.08 per 36 minutes (90% interval −0.01 to +0.18) and his steals by 0.00 (−0.03 to +0.03). Fouls are the exception: they move against usage, −0.12 per 36 (−0.17 to −0.06), about equally whether usage rises or falls.

7,390 season-to-season pairs, 1998-99 to 2025-26. Usage moves for reasons outside the player's control: the scoring talent added to or lost from his roster. Rising and falling halves are tested separately. Intervals: 90%, resampling players, with a correction for testing six stats. A one-night version (teammates absent) agrees, and adds a small lineup-defense cost on those nights.

Not supportedq-reverse-usage

Has the league become friendlier to traditional centers?

Not over the last five seasons as a whole. At fixed box-score prices, lineups with a traditional center (6-10 or taller, few threes) gained +0.24 points per 100 possessions in 2021-26 against 2014-21 (90% interval −0.43 to +0.95), and their pay premium at equal production fell by 2.6 points of the salary cap (1.5 to 3.7). The 2025-26 season alone is the exception (interval +2.7 to +5.1); the 2026-27 season will test whether it lasts.

Lineup stints, 2008-09 to 2025-26. Each lineup's results are compared with what its players' box scores predict at fixed prices, and the traditional-center share is tested by season; the declared contrast is 2021-26 against 2014-21. Pay: salary as a share of the cap at equal past production. Intervals: 90%, resampling team-seasons. Blind to: listed heights changed measurement standard around 2019.

Not supportedq-centers

Does moving the ball fast (short touches, many passes) make for a better offense?

No. These habits are real team traits: touch length carries over from season to season at r = 0.63 (90% interval 0.55 to 0.69). But across 390 team-seasons, one standard deviation more passes per 100 possessions goes with −0.43 points of offensive rating (−0.75 to −0.23), with pace and last season's offense held fixed; pace itself shows nothing (+0.03, −0.26 to +0.33).

NBA.com team tracking (touches, passes, drives), 2013-14 to 2025-26, with possessions and offensive rating from box scores. Intervals: 90%, resampling franchises. The likely reason runs the other way: teams with a star creator hold the ball and score well. Six traits were tested, so one chance result is possible.

Not supportedq-pace

After a drive and kick-out, what should the player who catches it do?

Shoot, by a small margin, or make one patient pass. In 2015-16 tracking data, with the situation at the catch held fixed, the catch-and-shoot three beats any pass by +0.08 points per kick-out (90% interval 0.00 to +0.16, just touching zero), down from a raw gap of +0.20. A pass after one to two seconds scores about as well as the shot, or better (by +0.11, interval −0.01 to +0.24), and holding the ball longer is the worst choice (the shot beats it by +0.18, +0.03 to +0.34).

Raw SportVU tracking (player and ball positions 25 times a second) for 631 games of 2015-16. The detector was tuned on 150 games, frozen, then checked against the NBA's own drive counts (player-by-player r = 0.97). The adjusted values come from a model that sees all ten players and the ball at the catch, combined with a model of who chooses what. Intervals: 90%, resampling games. Blind to: help rotations and defender intent after the catch; points on one possession are mostly noise.

Mixedq-drive-kick

How many NBA minutes until a player's early numbers explain most of his career peak?

Many more than 1,143 on our data. Scored on players no rating weight had seen, Singular's forecast explains 36% of the variation in career peak at 1,143 minutes (90% interval 25% to 42%). It reaches half only at about 3,680 minutes (2,414 to 4,701).

1,416 players who debuted 1997-98 to 2016-17 and reached age 29. Every rating behind a test player's forecast was fitted only on seasons before his checkpoint, and the peak is scored on a scale fitted before the test players arrived. Intervals: 90%, resampling players; in 24% of resamples the forecast never reaches 0.5 by 5,000 minutes.

Mixedq-peak-minutes

Player ratings and SiPM 136

What did SiPM's fitted weights learn about offense and defense?

Defense leans on lineup data (lineups carry 81% of the defensive projection, 73 to 88) and offense on the box score (40%, 35 to 46): the direction DARKO's published blend takes.

SupportedIn sample onlyDefense leans on lineup data (lineups carry 81% of the defensive projection, 73 to 88)E08

Does a team built from players' prior-season SiPM ratings, at the minutes they actually played, have the net rating the ratings imply?

Adding up each player's SiPM from the season before, weighted by the minutes he went on to play, tracks the team's actual net rating closely: r = 0.81 over 540 held-out team-seasons, with a slope near 1 (0.98). The test is given the real minutes, so it checks the per-possession ratings, not a minutes forecast. Updated 2026-10-15: was r = 0.80 between minutes-weighted team SiPM and actual team net rating; slope 0.98 (r 0.79 to 0.82; slope 0.93 to 1.04 (90%, bootstrap of whole seasons)) before SiPM rerun 2 (2026-10-09); numbers refreshed from the current output.

Sample: 540 team-seasons, regular-season stints

SupportedHeld outr = 0.81 between minutes-weighted team SiPM and actual team net rating; slope 0.98 (r 0.80 to 0.83; slope 0.93 to 1.04 (90%, bootstrap of whole seasons))SP06

Does the sum of five players' SiPM forecast a lineup's net rating, and how much noise sits around it?

Across 14,274 held-out lineups with 50+ possessions each way, the sum of the five players' prior-season SiPM correlates with the lineup's net rating at r = 0.29. Average results rise steadily from about -6 to +10 per 100 across the ten forecast groups, while single lineups spread over about 36 points per 100 in every group.

Sample: 14,274 lineup-seasons, median 170 possessions

SupportedHeld outpossession-weighted r = 0.29 between summed SiPM and lineup net rating (0.28 to 0.30 (90%, bootstrap of whole seasons))SP07

Does that result hold on other dates?

Level, not ahead. On every game day of 2025-26 (135 dates) SiPM is level with DARKO (+0.148, 90% -0.197 to +0.386 after the 2026-10-07 rerun), and level in the weekly replays of 2025-26, 2024-25 and 2023-24; also level with EPM, xRAPM and the public average.

Sample: 135 dates

Does SiPM (Singular Plus-Minus: RAPM v3 + a box model + an age curve) beat a fair baseline, the plain RAPM-and-BPM average given the same machinery?

Yes, narrowly: SiPM (rerun 2 since 2026-10-09) removes +0.025 percentage points more next-season lineup error than the fair bar (90% +0.016 to +0.034), ahead in 16 of 18 held-out seasons.

Does our lineup-based rating (RAPM) reproduce the best public version?

Yes. Our pooled 10-season RAPM ranks players like Engelmann's public 10-year RAPM: rank correlation 0.95 (882 players).

Sample: 882 players

How much of a player's next three seasons can public data know?

About 38% of future RAPM, tested forward in time; about 65% is predictable at all. So public data reaches about half of the knowable (57%, 48%, 50% by era since 1998, with missing futures dropped; 61% if they are filled at −2, a basis choice still open).

SupportedHeld outAbout 38% of future RAPM, tested forward in timeE39

What does each public data layer add, era by era?

The full box score adds +11 to +15 points of future-RAPM R-squared and lineup data +5 to +6, in every era since 1998, intervals above zero.

SupportedHeld outThe full box score adds +11 to +15 points of future-RAPM R-squared and lineup data +5 to +6, in every era since 1998, intervals above zeroE40a

Does SiPM's lineup-level edge show up when ranking individual players?

A tie: SiPM minus the plain RAPM-BPM average is +0.0071 in rank correlation (-0.0035 to +0.0179), below the smallest gap the test could detect (0.0164). Two seasons ahead +0.0128 (+0.0012 to +0.0251). Status changed 2026-10-15: next season is still a tie, but two seasons ahead SiPM now beats the plain average (+0.0128, +0.0012 to +0.0251, an interval that excludes zero), so the evidence is split. Updated 2026-10-15: was Spearman gap vs plain average: +0.0060 (-0.0048 to +0.0161) before the 2026-10-07 SiPM re-run; numbers refreshed from the current output.

Sample: 6,320 player-seasons, 1,295 players

At the top of SiPM's board, are non-creating bigs over-forecast relative to other players there?

When SiPM ranks a non-creating big in the season's top 30, his next-season impact comes in -0.36 points per 100 below the other top-30 players (-0.72 to -0.04) on current production (re-run 2026-10-10). It was -0.52 (-0.95 to -0.14) before the 2026-10-09 SiPM reruns (age term, one height standard, rim deterrence), which closed about a third of the gap. The rest of the cause is not found.

Sample: top 30: 540 rows, 82 NCB rows, 40 players

SupportedHeld out-0.36 points per 100 (top-30 NCB minus other top-30) (current production, re-run 2026-10-10; was -0.52) (-0.72 to -0.04 (90%; was -0.95 to -0.14))LR02

Is SiPM systematically wrong about high-usage players?

Not detectably. Held out over 18 seasons, high-usage players' lineups beat SiPM's forecast by +0.06 per 100 more than everyone else's (−0.07 to +0.20); with the forecast level held fixed, −0.01 (−0.14 to +0.13). SiPM's box-plus-lineup blend removes the lineup-only bias of SD02. Updated 2026-10-15: was +0.10 per 100 (high-usage minus rest) (90% player bootstrap −0.04 to +0.24) before the 2026-10-07 SiPM re-run; numbers refreshed from the current output.

Sample: 4,311 player-seasons, 947 players; high-usage = top 10% usage in t (469 rows)

Do lineup-based impact forecasts under-rate high-usage players' next-season on-court impact?

Held out, a forecast built only from our lineup rating (the analogue of EPM or xRAPM) left high-usage players' next-season lineups +0.29 per 100 better than forecast relative to everyone else (+0.15 to +0.43), mostly on offense (+0.25). A box-only forecast also under-forecast them (+0.21). For high-usage players whose box and lineup ratings disagree (box higher), which one predicts next season better?

The reverse. Does one postseason say much about a player beyond his regular season?

Not much: one postseason's lift over his regular season is about 4% player and the rest noise, and B15's lineup test gained 0.037% of the error. But a small riser trait is detectable over careers (RI01); the earlier line 'no separate playoff skill is large enough to detect' was withdrawn.

Not supportedClean nullletting playoff lineups move his rating improves held-out playoff predictions by a median 0.04% of the errorB15

Do some players reliably rise or fall in the playoffs?

Not across years: it holds within one playoff run (split-half 0.33) but does not repeat the next year (r 0.04, -0.01 to 0.09). Usage translation repeats weakly (r 0.17).

Within each height bucket, what separates the best players?

Scoring leads in every bucket. Only vision's share differs: smaller for guards than wings (−0.13, 90% −0.22 to −0.01; permutation p 0.008).

SupportedHeld outsmaller for guards than wings (−0.13, −0.22 to −0.01; permutation p 0.008)E18

Why does SiPM rank Durant about 105th when public models have him about 14th?

In 2024-25 Phoenix teammates shot +2.0 points per 100 above their usual rates with Durant on; SiPM treats that as luck and its chain carried 75% into this season. His Jan 6 lineup rating is 1.03 vs 4.21 on a plain three-season average, the biggest gap of 485 players. Without the luck adjustment he ranks 38th; without the over-35 age term as well, 12th.

SupportedIn sample onlyLineup rating 1.03 vs 4.21 (plain three-season); rank 106 -> 38 (no luck adj.) -> 12 (no luck, no age term)DD02

Has SiPM forecast a Durant decline each preseason that his lineup results did not show?

Partly, and not lately. His lineups beat the SiPM forecast in Brooklyn (2021-22 +3.04, 2022-23 +1.18 per 100). From 2023-24 they landed on it (−0.34, +0.11, −0.03) while the forecast fell from 5.0 to 2.2. Pooled 2023-24 to 2025-26: −0.50 (−2.25 to +1.69).

Sample: Residual RAPM for every player 2008-09 to 2025-26; Durant 200 game resamples per season

Do one-season drops in defensive lineup rating by players aged 21-25 persist?

About half persists. Players aged 21-25 whose one-season defensive RAPM fell 2+ points below their two prior seasons got back 0.56 of the drop next season (+0.49 to +0.63, 167 drops); 'half or more' is not determined. Older players and rises behave the same way: plain regression to the mean.

Sample: 455 drops of 2+ points, 167 at ages 21-25; 5563 rows in the all-rows regression

MixedIn sample onlyShare of the drop back next season 0.56 (90% player bootstrap 0.49 to 0.63)SD07

No. In the season after a 2+ point drop, young players' lineups defended −0.04 per 100 relative to SiPM's forecast compared with other young players (−0.25 to +0.18). SiPM already expects about half to come back. Does scaling SiPM's defensive part by k < 1 lower its error on next season's lineups?

No. Production (k = 1) is best. Keeping 90% of the defensive part changes the held-out score by −0.0019 (−0.0038 to −0.0001); keeping 75% by −0.0114 (−0.0160 to −0.0072). A k picked walk-forward also loses (−0.0022).

Does SiPM's luck adjustment earn its place?

Dropping the luck adjustment costs clear held-out accuracy (-0.0106, -0.0141 to -0.0069; ahead in only 3 of 18 seasons), even though it would put Durant at 40th. Durant's 2024-25 bonus did not repeat. Should SiPM's luck adjustment spare the part of teammate shooting that a player causes?

Adopted into production SiPM on 2026-10-06, on top of C12. Sparing the part of teammates' shooting a player causes scores level with C12 alone held out (-0.0001, -0.0005 to +0.0002) and level on the lineup rating's own next-season test (+0.0003, -0.0006 to +0.0011). It barely moves Durant (106th to 104th of 485 on January 6; 81st to 83rd in the 2026-27 projection). Updated 2026-10-15: was Held-out score minus current: -0.0001 (-0.0004 to +0.0003) before the 2026-10-07 SiPM re-run; numbers refreshed from the current output.

Does fading a luck correction faster improve SiPM?

Carrying one season's luck at about 0.33 instead of 0.75 moves flagged players most toward the public ranks (Durant 106 to 90) but passes the held-out rule by a hair (-0.0011, -0.0022 to +0.00003). It is an engineering choice, not a gravity finding.

Sample: 18 held-out seasons

MixedHeld outHeld-out score minus current: -0.0011 (-0.0022 to +0.00003)GR06

Does crediting defenders' effect on opponents' three-point residuals in the luck adjustment change held-out accuracy?

Letting each defender's estimated effect enter the expected rate of the threes he defends raises SiPM's held-out score: production G plus defenders minus G +0.0017 (+0.0008 to +0.0027); full joint J +0.0016 (+0.0007 to +0.0026). The offensive joint estimate alone is level (+0.0003). Ratings move little (2025-26 rank correlation 0.9994).

Sample: 18 held-out seasons; 500 paired game resamples

SupportedHeld outHeld-out score, G + defenders minus G: +0.0017 (+0.0008 to +0.0027)GR12

Adding the five defenders to the joint gravity model improves next-season prediction of three-point residuals (+0.0040, +0.0019 to +0.0062), but the defensive part does not follow players who change teams.

Does the gap between SiPM's box part and lineup part predict its next miss?

Players whose box part exceeds their lineup part keep falling short of SiPM; the reverse keep beating it. Each SD of the gap moves the next miss by -0.10 points per 100 (-0.14 to -0.06). Held out (added after the declared results), the gap alone cuts error by 0.41%.

Sample: 4,311 player-seasons, 947 players

SupportedHeld out-0.10 points per 100 per SD of gap (in sample); held-out MSE gain 0.0100 (-0.14 to -0.06; held out 0.0013 to 0.0195)PM03

If injury seasons are removed, do the same players stay persistently under- or over-forecast?

Mostly not. The named under-forecast players (Curry, Leonard, Hill, Embiid) beat the forecast in injury seasons too, and removing the injury effect makes their misses larger. On clean seasons alone the stable player share is 2.8% (shuffle null 95th percentile 3.3%, p 0.082); player means correlate 0.821 with the all-season means.

How much does the cross-validated penalty's move from 2,000 to 2,828 shrink 3-season RAPM, and what does it bias?

Yes. In the 10 changed seasons the top 30 players' 3-season RAPM is 0.886 of its one-penalty value; the spread is 1.86 / 1.39 (offense / defense) against 2.08 / 1.57. LR30's 'defensive spread fell' is this. It does not cause the young-big miss.

Sample: 30 seasons, 2 refits each

SupportedIn sample only0.886 = top-30 net 3-season RAPM at the production penalty / at one penalty (2,000), mean over 2015 and 2017-25 (0.881 to 0.893 (range across the 10 seasons; deterministic refit, no sampling interval))LR30e3

Does adding play-by-play rim inputs to SiPM's defensive box model improve held-out forecasts?

Sample: Rim shots 1996-97 to 2025-26 placed in lineup stints; tracking check 2013-14 to 2025-26 (6,942 player-seasons); T2 18 held-out seasons, 500 game and 500 team resamples; PL1 1,000 player resamples; young-big measure 400 team-season resamples.

Can a box-to-lineup value model be applied to seasons before lineup data exist?

Fitted on 2011-25 and tested on 1996-2010, the box model explains 0.63 of offensive and 0.38 of defensive 3-season RAPM variance (reverse: 0.56 / 0.32). The reduced 1951-72 version keeps offense (0.62) but loses most of defense (0.24).

Does production SiPM's box prior have the same top-end calibration gap?

Weaker and U-shaped: 3-season RAPM exceeds SiPM's walk-forward box prior by +0.26 (-0.02 to +0.53) per 100 in the prior's top decile (not distinguishable from zero) and by +0.54 (+0.44 to +0.66) in its bottom decile. The top-end gap sits with heavy-minute players and creators and is explained by exposure (RAPM is shrunk more for low-minute players), not a star effect. The final SiPM forecast under-rates its top decile (+0.19 (+0.02 to +0.34) next season, +0.43 (+0.08 to +0.74) three seasons) whatever the box prior.

Sample: 11,929 player-seasons

MixedHeld outTop box-prior decile, 3-season RAPM minus box value: +0.26 per 100 (-0.02 to +0.53 (90%, 1,000 player resamples))AG22

Do the three changes tested in sandboxes on 2026-10-09, together or in subsets, beat production SiPM on held-out lineups without hurting players?

Yes: SiPM with these changes removes +4.10 more per 1,000 of next-season lineup error than the previous production SiPM (90% +2.20 to +6.06), ahead in 11 of 18 held-out seasons, and changes next-season player squared error by -0.68% (-1.03 to -0.30), a gain. The lineup-rating prior part (age, heights) carries the steady lineup gain; the season-scaled box stats carry the player gain. Adopted 2026-10-09.

Sample: 18 held-out seasons of lineup stints; player tests 6,458 next-season and 4,365 three-season player-seasons; young-big measure 18 seasons

Should the single-season RAPM fit, the target of V3's box prior, also move to one penalty?

No: with V3's prior fitted on single-season RAPM at one penalty (2,000) instead of the per-season choice, SiPM's held-out lineup forecast is level or slightly worse (-0.86 per 1,000, -1.65 to +0.02) and its next-season player error is worse (+0.12% (+0.01 to +0.22)). Trajectory, aging and breakouts 153

Can a before-the-season forecast of each dimension of a player's season be calibrated?

Forecasting each player's efficiency, usage, creation, minutes, availability and value separately, walk-forward, its 80% ranges cover 78-84% of outcomes. Efficiency R-squared is 0.61, against 0.44 for last season alone.

Sample: About 5,900 player-seasons per dimension, walk-forward

SupportedHeld outEfficiency R-squared 0.62 (last season only 0.44); 80% coverage 78-84% (0.58 to 0.65 (R-squared))TB01

How predictable are breakouts, and when do they happen?

The baseline expected a median 17% of an efficiency breakout and 10% of a value breakout. Value breakouts fall from about 7.2 per 100 seasons at 21 and under to 0.3 at 31 and over.

Sample: 108 efficiency, 197 value breakouts

SupportedIn sample onlyValue breakouts per 100 seasons: 7.2 (21-) to 0.3 (31+) (5.0 to 10.0 (21-))TB05

Which knowable causes mark efficiency and value breakouts?

Young high picks (picks 1-10 in seasons 2-4) are 4.97x over-represented among impact breakouts and 3.61x among value breakouts. A new team is under-represented (0.53x and 0.36x).

Sample: 108 impact and 197 value breakouts

SupportedIn sample onlyYoung high pick lift: impact 4.97, value 3.61; new team lift: impact 0.53, value 0.36 (young high pick share 0.40 (0.32 to 0.48); new team share 0.16 (0.11 to 0.22) (impact) vs base 0.08, 0.296)FM12

Were the biggest usage and creation surprises predictable from team context?

Most of the largest usage surprises had a specific cause known before the season: a star teammate left or was out at the start, the player became the top option, he returned from a lost season, or his team had a top-decile opening. 40 of the top 50 usage surprises had one, against 47% of all player-seasons.

Sample: Walk-forward baseline rows

SupportedIn sample onlyUsage top 50: 40 of 50 with a specific cause (48% of all eligible seasons); creation 37 of 50; promoted-to-top-option lift 3.9 (usage), 6.5 (creation); star teammate left 1.9 and 2.0FM01

Which knowable causes mark usage and creation breakouts?

Among one-season usage breakouts, a star teammate leaving appears 2.15x as often as in all player-seasons, promotion to top option 2.37x and a top-decile opening 2.42x. Creation breakouts show the same pattern. Descriptive tags, not causes.

Which traits decline with age, and when?

Rim shots, dunks, fast-break shots, blocks and offensive rebounds peak at 21-24 and slide from there; shooting, passing and ball security hold until about 26-28. From 27 to 31 the explosive group falls 0.13 league spreads more than the skill group (-0.17 to -0.08).

Do breakouts on different dimensions happen together?

Usage and creation break out together 12.8 times as often as chance (10.3 to 15.5); efficiency and usage 4.4 times (2.8 to 6.3). Brunson broke out on usage and creation in 2022-23, then on efficiency, usage and value in 2023-24.

Sample: 4,673 seasons eligible on three dimensions

SupportedIn sample onlyLift usage+creation 12.8x; efficiency+usage 4.4x (11.0 to 15.3; 2.7 to 6.2)TB07

Are staircase risers a distinct, identifiable group?

Yes, with no window cap. About 1 in 8 players beat their draft-slot path on efficiency at every draft range; 15% of plateaus are followed by a rise beyond forecast, often on another dimension; early underperformers rarely recover. Rolling outperformance is no more common than chance (TB24).

Sample: 1,135 draft-scored players; 2,184 plateaus; 223 early underperformers

SupportedHeld outDraft outperformers 145 of 1,135 (efficiency); plateau rises 335 of 2,184; early slumps recovered 5%; 744 players in any of ten types (3% to 8% (early recovery))U01

Does the breakout definition catch slow-grind improvers?

Kawhi Leonard and Jimmy Butler never cross the efficiency bar in one season: their efficiency rose 2 to 2.7 points per 100 in several seasons, each below the 3.1 bar. They show up only on usage, creation, minutes and value.

Of 2,184 plateaus, 336 were followed by a rise beyond forecast (15%); in 249 the rise was on another dimension (e.g. Butler's usage after 2012-14). The chance falls from 42% (38-46%) for plateaus ending at 23 and under to 4% (3-5%) at 30+.

Is rolling outperformance a real type (climber type 4)?

Not above chance: runs of seasons above the one-season forecast happen as often as when each player's seasons are shuffled (efficiency 32 vs 39 by chance). The baseline already absorbs persistent outperformance.

Do breakouts last?

Under the design's talent definition, 106 player-seasons broke out from 1997-98 to 2025-26 (about 3.7 a season), and 58 of the 106 held up the next season. A quick read-only look, no interval.

Does the precedent lookup add forecasting information beyond age?

Held out, the share of a player's 40 nearest historical precedents who broke out next season ranks players no better than the breakout rate for his age band (AUC 0.701 vs 0.708). The lookup is a reading aid, not a better forecast.

Not supportedClean nullHeld-out AUC 0.701 (precedents) vs 0.708 (age band); Brier 0.0606 vs 0.0609 (AUC difference -0.0251 to 0.0118; Brier gain -0.000427 to 0.000963 (90% player bootstrap))FM10

Used as an input rather than a replacement, does the precedent share help?

Added to age in a walk-forward logistic, the fitted precedent share raises held-out AUC by +0.018 for next-season breakouts and +0.065 for a breakout or climb within three seasons; it still adds on top of age plus the baseline.

Sample: 6,365 and 5,040 queries

SupportedHeld out+0.023 AUC (one season), +0.066 (three seasons) over age; +0.013 / +0.049 over age + baseline (one season +0.016 to +0.030; three seasons +0.053 to +0.079; over age + baseline +0.007 to +0.019 / +0.038 to +0.060 (90% player bootstrap; Brier gains also above zero))PF02

Do learned similarity weights make the precedent breakout share a better forecast than age alone?

It does not. Weights learned walk-forward to predict breakouts, climbs and value changes over the next 1-3 seasons give a held-out breakout AUC of 0.695 vs 0.708 for age alone; no fitted variant beats age (best 0.711).

Sample: 7,356 queries, 1,358 players; breakout rate 6.7%

Not supportedClean nullHeld-out AUC 0.706 (fitted) vs 0.708 (age band); Brier 0.06060 vs 0.06085 (AUC difference -0.017 to +0.014; Brier gain -0.00047 to +0.00087 (90% player bootstrap, 500))U29

Does the fitted lookup beat the trajectory baseline at ranking who breaks out next season?

Against the declared baseline comparator (forecast turned into a probability with its spread, AUC 0.580) the fitted lookup wins, but that comparator is weak. Against a fairer baseline score added after the first run (logistic on the forecast gaps, AUC 0.697) it ties.

Sample: 7,356 queries, 1,358 players; breakout rate 6.7%

Not supportedClean nullAUC 0.706 vs 0.697 (fair baseline); vs declared comparator +0.125 (vs fair baseline -0.011 to +0.032; vs declared +0.103 to +0.149 (90% player bootstrap))PF01

Can the lookup replace the baseline for value forecasts?

It does not. Predicting next season's change from the precedents' changes beats age alone but falls short of the baseline forecast (R² imp 0.14 vs 0.18, usg 0.11 vs 0.23, cre_pct 0.08 vs 0.18, mpg 0.23 vs 0.27, value 0.13 vs 0.18).

Sample: 4,720 queries (usage)

Not supportedHeld outusage R² 0.11 (fitted lookup) vs 0.23 (baseline) (fitted minus baseline -0.135 to -0.101 (variance units, 90% player bootstrap))PF04

Does the lookup forecast development over 1-3 seasons better than age?

On its own, the fitted lookup's share of precedents with a breakout or climber event within three seasons gives held-out AUC 0.724 vs 0.663 for age; the current lookup gives 0.720. Updated 2026-10-15: was AUC 0.725 vs 0.664 (difference +0.047 to +0.075 (90% player bootstrap, 200)) before the 2026-10-05/07 re-runs; numbers refreshed from the current output.

How much of a young high pick's growth was expected from draft day?

For a top-3 pick, the baseline expected a median 54% of his breakout-sized minutes rise; for picks 31+, 30%. For efficiency and value, even top picks' breakouts were mostly unforeseen. Added after the first results.

Sample: About 5,900 player-seasons per dimension, walk-forward

Can anyone predict a late (25+) jump in three-point volume (the Brook Lopez question)?

No. Lasting jumps are rare (111 of 4,025 seasons at 25+, 2.8%), and nothing measured in advance predicts them: out-of-sample AUC 0.56 vs 0.54 for season and prior volume alone.

Is a rookie's second-half jump real?

Mostly not: about 85% noise. Splitting his games odd and even produces almost the same spread.

Not supportedIn sample onlyabout 85% noiseT22

Where does a departing player's share of plays go over a season?

Yes. Of the share of team plays that departing players leave, arrivals take 87.1% and the players who stay take 12.9% (11.6 to 14.2). Pre-season veteran arrivals take 47.4%, rookies 23.1%, in-season arrivals 16.6%.

Sample: 862 team-season transitions, 8,161 arrivals, 6,163 stayer rows

SupportedIn sample onlystayers 12.9% of departed plays; arrivals 87.1% (stayers 11.6 to 14.2 (90%, team-season bootstrap))UA16

When stayers' share of team plays rises after departures, is it a bigger role per game or more games?

Of departed plays, stayers' role growth (share of plays in the games they play) is 4.3% and their availability growth (playing more of the team's games) 8.6%.

Sample: 862 team-season transitions, 8,161 arrivals, 6,163 stayer rows

SupportedIn sample onlyrole part 4.3%, availability part 8.6% of departed plays (role 3.2 to 5.3; availability 7.4 to 9.9)UD01

When a high-usage player leaves, does his share go to the remaining players the way it does on nights he sits?

No. Held out on 2013-14 to 2025-26 departures, splitting the opening with the game-level model changes season forecast error 0.4% vs no allocation, and ranks within teams worse than the proportional split. Stayers pick up only 11% of a departure's share combined. Updated 2026-10-15: was season error 0.4% (game-level split) and -0.1% (proportional) vs drift only (-1.1 to 2.0%; -1.4 to 1.2%; stayers' combined share of the opening 8 to 13%) before the 2026-10-05 game-order fix re-run; numbers refreshed from the current output.

Does a team play faster or slower (more or fewer plays per game) to absorb a departure?

No. In per-game terms the change in the team's total plays covers -0.7% of departed plays; arrivals 87.3%, stayers 13.4%.

Does a team-level replacement-plan signal predict how much stayers absorb on held-out seasons?

Yes. Adding the arrivals' predicted share (from prior shares and draft slots) cuts the error in predicting the stayers' role growth by 8.4% for plays and 11.0% for created baskets on 2013-14 to 2025-26.

SupportedHeld outMSE change -8.4% vs opening only (plays) (-13.7 to -2.5% (90%, team-season bootstrap))UD10

Do stayers absorb more when the arrivals are weak relative to the opening?

Yes. When the arrivals' predicted share covers little of the opening (bottom third), stayers' role growth is 10.9% of departed plays; when it covers the opening (top third), -3.5%.

Sample: 862 team-season transitions, 8,161 arrivals, 6,163 stayer rows

SupportedIn sample onlylow minus high coverage: 14.4% of departed plays (stayers' role part) (11.9 to 17.0)UD03

Do stayers absorb more when the arrivals are few?

Weakly. With 0-3 pre-season arrivals stayers' role growth is 7.3% of departed plays; with 6+, 3.0%.

Sample: 862 team-season transitions, 8,161 arrivals, 6,163 stayer rows

SupportedIn sample onlyfew minus many arrivals: 4.3% (0.7 to 7.9)UD04

How much does a role opening add to a player's usage beyond the forecast?

Only top-decile openings push usage above the forecast (+0.41, 0.23 to 0.59) and creation (+0.66). Sharing an opening in proportion to current usage over-predicts badly (usage MSE 6.19 to 47.7).

Do stayers absorb more when the departure is a star (top-1 usage) rather than a role player?

Do Griffin's examples of stayers who exploded fit a describable pattern, or are they outliers?

Partly. Brown 2025-26, Westbrook 2016-17 and Harden 2024-25 are all heirs (the top claim after the top option left); heirs are 3.68x over-represented among the 50 largest usage jumps. Kobe 2005-06 sits only in the large-opening cell, with no top-option departure (his 2004-05 jump, after the Shaq trade, is in the 'top option stays, co-star left' cell).

Does explosive reliance predict exits rather than decline among stayers?

58% of explosive-reliant players are gone by 33 against 50% of skill-reliant ones. The evidence for Griffin's idea sits in who leaves, not how fast stayers decline. When does each skill peak, and how fast does it fall?

Blocks, offensive rebounds and steals peak by 23; overall value (BPM) at 26; assists 27; 3P%, FT%, 3PA at 28 or later. After 31 the steepest falls are shot volume, free throws and true shooting.

SupportedIn sample onlyBlocks, offensive rebounds and steals peak by 23T03

Does the age drift of the latent level depend on career stage and pre-NBA years, not only on age?

At about 23, a second-year player (entered at 22.5) is expected to gain +0.30 impact per 100 next season; a fifth-year player of the same age is flat (0.00). Growth stops around 23-26 for early entrants and decline starts near 27.

Sample: Full-sample state-space fit

SupportedIn sample onlyEfficiency drift at ~23: 2nd-year +0.30 vs 5th-year 0.00 per season; at 30: -0.25; at 35: -0.36 (0.22 to 0.38 (2nd-year); -0.06 to 0.05 (5th-year); 90% jackknife over 10 player groups)SS07

Does composition-adjusted foul-drawing skill fall less with age than raw FT rate?

About half. From 24 to 35, shooting fouls per attempt fall 30% of the league rate for the typical player, and shot mix accounts for 47% of that. Composition dominates at 24-27 (about 90%); at 31-35 the decline is mostly lost foul drawing (FT05).

Sample: 200 player bootstraps

SupportedIn sample onlyComposition share of the 24-35 decline: 47% (player-average); 58% (pooled, declared) (0.41 to 0.54 (player-average); 0.46 to 0.71 (pooled))FT02

Does FT-based explosiveness still fall with age once shot mix is fixed?

Mix-adjusted foul drawing peaks at 27 (raw FT rate at 24) and falls 0.33 league spreads from 31 to 35 (-0.44 to -0.24), about as much as raw FT rate (-0.30). So the late decline is not composition (declared H3b not supported).

Sample: 200 player bootstraps

SupportedIn sample onlySkill change 31-35: -0.33 spreads; peak age 27; skill minus raw FT rate 31-35: -0.03 (-0.44 to -0.24; peak 24 to 28; -0.11 to +0.04)FT05

Does the make/miss mix change the free-throw count with age?

From 31 to 35, and-ones per make fall 0.31 league spreads (-0.47 to -0.15), so free throws per shooting foul rise +0.22 (0.05 to 0.46). Raw FT rate hides part of the finishing decline. Updated 2026-10-15: was And-ones per make 31-35: -0.31 spreads; FTs per shooting foul +0.22 (-0.47 to -0.15; 0.04 to 0.46) before the re-runs after 2026-10-08 (SiPM reruns and the ordered re-run of 2026-10-09/10); numbers refreshed from the current output.

SupportedIn sample onlyAnd-ones per make 31-35: -0.31 spreads; FTs per shooting foul +0.22 (-0.47 to -0.15; 0.05 to 0.46)FT06

Which granularity of injury category is worth carrying in a forecast?

For gap returners (Klay Thompson 2021-22, Durant 2020-21), does propagating the latent level through the gap beat persistence?

Are the misses on a returning star's efficiency, minutes and games correlated?

Within rise episodes, does efficiency lead usage?

Where both rose, efficiency crossed first in 38% of 128 episodes (chance 34%); usage first in 23% (chance 31%). Efficiency first is inside the chance band; usage first is at its low edge.

Not supportedClean nullEfficiency first 38% vs chance 33% (31% to 46%; chance band 27% to 40%)TS02

Do minutes (role) move before usage?

Minutes per game rose early before 44% of usage breakouts vs 18% of controls. Over all players, a minutes rise with usage flat raises next season's chance of a usage rise from 9% to 18%.

Sample: 10,228 player-seasons, 1,820 players

SupportedIn sample only+25.6 points early-move share (usage breakouts); +8.8 points next-season usage rise (+17.7 to +36.0; +6.7 to +11.2)TS04

When usage and creation both rise, which moves first?

In 100 rise episodes where both rose, usage and creation crossed in the same season 60% of the time; random timing gives 35%. Usage first (19%) and creation first (21%) are both below chance.

Sample: 10,228 player-seasons, 1,820 players

SupportedIn sample only60% same season vs 36% by chance (51% to 67% (90%, player bootstrap); chance band 28% to 43%)TS01

Does a usage rank far below efficiency close from below?

At the same usage quintile, players whose efficiency ranks 2+ quintiles higher are no more likely to gain a usage quintile (-0.2 points). They are more likely to gain minutes (+9.3).

Sample: 10,228 player-seasons, 1,820 players

Not supportedClean null-0.2 points (-4.1 to +3.2)TS06

Does a usage rank far above efficiency close from above?

At the same usage quintile, players whose efficiency ranks 2+ quintiles lower are +12.7 points more likely to drop a usage quintile.

Can a model give calibrated probabilities for each kind of breakout over the next one to three seasons, for every player, better than age and draft slot?

A walk-forward logistic model gives every player the chance of seven breakout types (jump or multi-season climb) and of a first starter-level or star-level season within 1-3 seasons. Pooled over the seven types, its three-season forecasts beat age plus draft slot by 4.2% in Brier score (3.3% to 5.3%); any-breakout AUC 0.76 vs 0.72. The probabilities are calibrated.

Which traits stay stable when a player's role changes?

Does shooting rise when a player's role grows?

Role changers' FT% (+1.79, 1.41 to 2.17), 3P% and pull-up eFG rise against same-role players. FT% barely depends on role, so its rise suggests players given more role were already improving: selection. The test cannot tell selection from flow.

Sample: 1,286 vs 3,764 pairs

MixedIn sample onlyFT% level shift for role changers: +1.79 points (1.41 to 2.17)TB13

How much of free-throw rate is shot mix rather than foul-drawing skill?

Yes. Most free throws come from fouled missed twos, and paint and rim attempts draw shooting fouls far more often than jumpers. A foul-drawing measure that holds shot mix fixed is nearly as stable as raw FT rate and reorders players: raw FT rate flatters ball-handling guards with bonus FTs and rim-heavy bigs.

Sample: 3,759 pairs

SupportedIn sample onlyMix-adjusted skill year-to-year r 0.81 (raw FT rate 0.85) (0.79 to 0.83)FT01

Where do free throws come from, by foul type and shot type?

Fouled missed twos give 63% of free-throw attempts, bonus fouls 18% (23% in 1996-2015), and-ones 10%, fouled threes 5%. Shooting fouls per attempt: paint 0.19, rim 0.16, mid-range 0.07, threes 0.01.

Sample: No sampling interval (league totals)

SupportedIn sample onlyFTA share: fouled missed 2 63%, bonus 18%, and-one 10%, fouled missed 3 5%; shooting fouls per attempt rim 0.16, paint 0.19, mid 0.07, three 0.01FT03

Can foul-drawing skill be separated from shot mix?

Shooting fouls drawn against what a player's shot mix implies is nearly as stable year to year as raw FT rate (r 0.81 vs 0.85) and robust to the allocation and location choices (correlations 0.999 and 0.958 with alternatives).

Sample: 3,759 pairs

SupportedIn sample onlyYear-to-year r 0.81 (raw FT rate 0.85); rank correlation with a contest-adjusted version 0.985 (0.79 to 0.83)FT04

Creation and on-ball load 52

When a player's usage rises because a teammate is out, how much does his efficiency change?

When a teammate's absence hands a player one more point of usage, his true shooting falls 0.52 points (0.42 to 0.61). The result holds for injury-only absences, in every era, and in a plain with/without split.

Sample: 561,831 player-games, 10,401 player-team-seasons, 892 team-seasons; intervals resample team-seasons

SupportedIn sample onlyTS -0.52 per usage point; points per 100 plays -0.81 (-0.61 to -0.42 (TS))UE01

Does usage moved by season-level roster changes cost the same as usage moved by a teammate's absence (UE01)?

Yes. Instrumenting a player's season usage with roster competition, team openings, absences and coach changes gives 0.59 TS points per usage point on the 2022-23 to 2025-26 core; the game-level absence method on the same seasons gives 0.54.

Sample: 1,533 player-team-seasons, 564 players; 90% intervals from a player-cluster bootstrap (400)

SupportedHeld out0.59 TS points per usage point (season level); game level 0.54 (0.36 to 0.77; 0.35 to 0.74)UC01

Which part of a player's usage change carries the efficiency cost in next-season forecasts?

Yes, held out. In next-season TS forecasts, the usage change explained by roster, opening, absences, coach and the player's shot habit costs about 0.35 per usage point; the unexplained rest costs -0.01. Separating them cuts error 2.7% against one cost on the whole change; splitting asked from chosen adds nothing (-0.05%). Applying UE01's 0.52 to every usage change makes forecasts 18% worse. (Numbers above are from the 2019-20-onward run. Re-run on 2022-23 to 2025-26: MSE gain, explained/unexplained split vs one cost: 3.5%; fixed 0.52 on all change: -36%.)

Sample: 237 test rows, 237 players; paired player bootstrap (500)

SupportedHeld outMSE gain, explained/unexplained split vs one cost: 3.5%; fixed 0.52 on all change: -36% (0.0 to 6.6%; -53 to -20%)UC05

Does the efficiency cost of more usage depend on what kind of player he is?

Per usage point gained, spacers lose 1.26 TS points and on-ball creators 0.16; the declared contrast is -1.10 (-1.54 to -0.71). High free-throw shooters and self-creators lose less.

Sample: 561,831 player-games, 10,401 player-team-seasons, 892 team-seasons; intervals resample team-seasons

SupportedIn sample onlySpacer minus creator: -1.10 TS per usage point (-1.54 to -0.71)UE02

When usage rises, does a player get worse, or is he handed worse shots?

About 69% of the drop in points per shot is a worse shot diet and the rest is making fewer of the same shots (label-safe shot model; 64% with the published model). Tracking data (2020-26) confirm the shots get more contested.

Sample: 5.9M priced shots

SupportedIn sample onlyPer 10 points of missing usage: shot value -0.38, shot-making -0.17 per 100 shots (label-safe model) (-0.41 to -0.34 (value); -0.25 to -0.09 (making); diet share 0.69 (0.60 to 0.80))UE04

Why do the obvious usage-efficiency comparisons show little cost?

Across players the slope is flat (-0.005 TS per usage point, -0.037 to +0.031) and within a player from season to season it is small (-0.067). Coaches give the ball to players who can carry it, which hides the cost the absence experiment shows.

Sample: 7,146 player-seasons

SupportedIn sample onlyCross-player slope -0.005 TS per usage point (-0.037 to +0.031)UE13

Is the efficiency cost from taking more shots, or from losing the teammate who created them?

Hold fixed how much passing left the floor, and the cost of the extra shots alone shrinks to -0.06 TS per usage point (-0.21 to +0.08), not distinguishable from zero. Players lose efficiency when the guy who set them up is gone.

Sample: 561,831 player-games, 10,401 player-team-seasons, 892 team-seasons; intervals resample team-seasons

SupportedIn sample onlyCost of extra shots with passing held fixed: -0.06 TS per usage point; lost passing -0.18 TS per 10 points of missing assist share (-0.21 to +0.08 (shots); -0.25 to -0.10 (passing))UE03

When a high-usage player sits, does each teammate gain in proportion to his current share?

No: the split is about halfway between equal and proportional. A teammate's share of what the star leaves rises with his current share at 0.45 of the proportional slope. Held out, the strict proportional rule explains 3% of who gains what; a fitted usage-only model 10%.

Sample: 368 absence events, 308 team-seasons, 4,433 teammate rows; held out = fit 1996-2012, test 2013-2025

Not supportedHeld outslope of absorption fraction on proportional share 0.46 (1 = proportional, 0 = equal) (0.41 to 0.51 (90%, team-season bootstrap))UA01

Does a teammate's creation skill predict how much of a sitting star's usage he takes?

Yes. Unassisted scorers absorb more of the star's share; dropping creation traits raises held-out error 2.8%. Per SD of the creation composite, a teammate gains 1.2 more points of the star's share, mostly through on-court usage rate.

Sample: 368 absence events, 308 team-seasons, 4,433 teammate rows; held out = fit 1996-2012, test 2013-2025

SupportedHeld out+1.27 points of the star's share per SD of creation; unassisted share +1.0 per SD (0.92 to 1.62 (creation); 0.56 to 1.46 (unassisted); held-out drop 1.9 to 3.7%)UA03

When the lead creator sits, who takes over his shots?

Last season's creation score (θ) predicts who absorbs the usage (+0.27 usage per 36 per SD, 0.17 to 0.36) beyond prior usage, but not who keeps his efficiency; prior usage alone flags step-ups better (68% vs 62%). 2,213 teammate cases, 289 team-seasons.

Sample: 289 team-seasons

MixedHeld outLast season's creation score (θ) predicts who absorbs the usage (+0.27 usage per 36 per SD, 0.17 to 0.36)T05

Does tracking-data creation predict absorption beyond box-score creation?

Small. Last season's drives and pull-ups per 36 cut held-out error 1.3% (0.2 to 2.4%) once box-score creation traits are in (after the 2026-10-07 game-order fix; before: -0.8%, interval including zero). Status changed 2026-10-15: the held-out error change (-1.3%, 90% -2.4 to -0.2%) excludes zero, so tracked creation does add to the forecast; the gain is small. The 2026-10-07 entry had left this call open. Updated 2026-10-15: was held-out error change -1.2% (leave-one-season-out, 2013-14 on) (-2.4 to -0.2%) before the 2026-10-05/07 re-runs; numbers refreshed from the current output.

Sample: 368 absence events, 308 team-seasons, 4,433 teammate rows; held out = fit 1996-2012, test 2013-2025

How do players become their team's lead creator?

Of 203 seasons where a player became his team's top creator, 45 were creation breakouts, 27 had a usage breakout that season or the one before, and 61 followed a move to a new team. Most inherited the role at a modest level after a teammate left.

Sample: 203 seasons

SupportedIn sample only45 of 203 new lead creators were creation breakoutsTB18

Does the stayer who becomes the top remaining creator grab more of the opening?

Yes. 'Heirs' (highest prior creation share on the opening roster, not the old top option) gain 2.4 points of team created baskets on average, vs 0.1 for other stayers; 33% gain 5+ points.

Sample: 862 team-season transitions, 8,161 arrivals, 6,163 stayer rows

SupportedIn sample onlyM2 opening x heir +0.107 (0.007 to 0.188 (90%, team-season bootstrap))UD07

Do certain players consistently create 'lucky' teammate shooting, or is it luck?

Teammates' three-point luck carries over for players who change teams (r 0.040, 0.006 to 0.075), beating shuffle and noise nulls. A typical player's true effect is about 0.47 pp on each teammate three (0.18 to 0.65), about 0.41 points per 100 possessions. The free-throw placebo shows nothing.

Sample: 2,458 season pairs; 1,195 players in the multi-season estimate

SupportedHeld outTeam-changer season-to-season r 0.040; true effect SD 0.47 pp per teammate three (0.006 to 0.075 (r); 0.18 to 0.65 (pp))GR01

On which shot types does the teammate effect show?

For team changers, above-the-break persistence is r 0.056 (0.020 to 0.093) and corner threes 0.000 (-0.038 to 0.037). Corner threes are almost all catch-and-shoot, so this is not a clean catch-and-shoot gravity result.

Sample: 2,458 season pairs

SupportedHeld outAbove-the-break r 0.056; corner r 0.000 (0.020 to 0.093; -0.038 to 0.037)GR03

Do teammates shoot better with the lead creator on the floor (the Curry lift)?

Teammates shoot +1.2 TS points better in games he plays. Per-player estimates are mostly noise, and the effect of who replaces him is not separated.

SupportedIn sample onlyTeammates shoot +1.2 TS points better in games he playsE28

How much of one season's teammate shooting lift is signal?

One season of the teammate-shooting measure is about 98% sampling noise; the multi-season estimate's uncertainty (about +/-0.34 pp) is larger than most gaps between players.

Sample: 200 simulated seasons at expected rates

SupportedIn sample onlyShare of one season that is noise: about 98%GR02

Does the gravity estimate split credit correctly between teammates who share the floor?

Supported, but small. Fitting all players on the floor at once moves about 18% of the top players' raw teammate lift to their teammates (+0.24 pp, +0.20 to +0.28) and predicts next season better, yet the list barely reorders (rank correlation 0.985). Murray stays above Jokic (0.72 vs 0.60 pp); the gap's interval (-0.28 to +0.47) cannot say which of them carries it.

Sample: 1.6M threes; held-out targets 2002-03 to 2025-26

SupportedHeld outJOD minus marginal, held out: +0.0038 % of squared error; top-30 teammate part +0.24 pp (+0.0018 to +0.0059 (held out); +0.20 to +0.28 (teammate part))GR07

Is a non-scorer's teammate shooting lift explained by screens and passing rather than gravity?

Mixed. Across 518 players (2016-26), joint gravity tracks potential assists (+0.077 pp per SD, +0.059 to +0.095, height fixed), not scoring volume. Screen assists link to it only through height (+0.018, -0.009 to +0.043). Draymond: joint +0.47 pp, rank 7 of 518; his passing and screening profile predicts +0.26.

Sample: 518 players; 1,000 player resamples

MixedIn sample onlyPotential assists +0.077 pp per SD; screen assists +0.018 (height fixed) (+0.059 to +0.095; -0.009 to +0.043)GR08

Does the efficiency cost of usage depend on whether the team asked for it?

Not shown. On the 2022-23 to 2025-26 core (pull-up labels reliable), usage the roster hands a player costs 0.59 TS points per usage point and usage he takes himself costs 0.30. The gap, 0.29 (-0.14 to 0.65), includes zero; the direction does not hold in both halves, and the held-out prediction no longer beats predicting no change.

Sample: 1,533 player-team-seasons, 564 players; 90% intervals from a player-cluster bootstrap (400)

Not supportedHeld outasked 0.59 vs chosen 0.30 TS points lost per usage point; gap 0.29 (asked 0.36 to 0.77; chosen 0.01 to 0.64; gap -0.14 to 0.65)U02

Is discretionary (self-chosen) shot volume a stable player trait?

Yes. Early-clock pull-ups, step-backs and fadeaways above his team's norm correlate 0.92 between halves of a season and 0.87 year to year. (Numbers above are from the 2019-20-onward run. Re-run on 2022-23 to 2025-26: year-to-year r 0.89; split-half r 0.93.)

Sample: 1,533 player-team-seasons, 843 consecutive-season pairs; player bootstrap (400)

SupportedHeld outyear-to-year r 0.89; split-half r 0.93 (0.87 to 0.91; 0.91 to 0.94)UC02

Is on-court yield (design 3) stable enough to measure a player's choice?

No. A player's share of shots with a better teammate on versus off correlates 0.36 within a season but only 0.16 year to year; it mostly reflects the teammates and the minutes structure. (Numbers above are from the 2019-20-onward run. Re-run on 2022-23 to 2025-26: year-to-year r 0.14; split-half r 0.34.)

Does chosen usage travel with a player beyond what his old usage already says?

No. For 533 team changes, adding the old-team chosen-shot excess and yield to old usage and the new roster changes held-out error by -0.9% (-2.0 to +0.4); old usage already carries the habit. (Numbers above are from the 2019-20-onward run. Re-run on 2022-23 to 2025-26: held-out MSE gain -1.2%; chosen-excess coefficient +0.17 usage points per point.)

Priced against the team alternative, do a player's excess self-chosen plays cost points?

Not on average. Excess discretionary plays are small (about 0.39 per 100 team plays) and score about what teammates would: -0.01 points a season (-0.25 to +0.25) on non-rebuilding teams. Rebuilding teams (own category): +0.34 (-0.01 to +0.71). (Numbers above are from the 2019-20-onward run. Is Griffin's case (usage grows while efficiency drops only a little) common?

Of 227 role breakouts (usage or minutes), 197 (87%) kept impact within 1 point per 100 of the player's past or raised it. Usage and efficiency surprises move together (r 0.24), so roles grow mostly when a player is playing well.

Does the return treatment change SiPM's box rating forecast?

Yes. No method moves its per-game error by more than a fraction of a percent (-0.11% at best). The box model weighs makes against attempts, so a pull toward average volume cancels. Durant: points per 36 fell from 26.2 to 21.0 at the return, box rating +6.43 vs realized +6.18.

Lineups, fit and portability 13

Which player types fit together in lineups?

Next to a star creator, spacers add +0.38 per 100 (0.26 to 0.54) and interior bigs +0.29; a second star creator costs −0.90 (−1.21 to −0.56). 12 of 28 pair cells exclude zero (2.8 by chance), and pair effects fitted on 1996-2013 carry into 2013-26 at 1.19 of their size (0.69 to 1.69).

SupportedReplicated out of sampleNext to a star creator, spacers add +0.38 per 100 (0.26 to 0.54)B18

Held out one season at a time, a lead creator lifts lineups +1.41 per 100 beyond the ratings (0.75 to 2.07). Shooters −0.23 (−0.53 to +0.05) on a career rule; spacing centers add nothing measurable.

SupportedHeld outHeld out one season at a time, a lead creator lifts lineups +1.41 per 100 beyond the ratings (0.75 to 2.07)B21

Do switchable defenders make lineups better than their ratings?

Switchability is a stable trait (reliability 0.71). Lineups with more switchable defenders allow 0.28 fewer points per 100 per SD (0.12 to 0.45) than ratings predict, also with prior-season switchability.

SupportedHeld outLineups with more switchable defenders allow 0.28 fewer points per 100 per SD (0.12 to 0.45)B20

Does a champion need a star creator?

Teams with one won 32 titles where the bracket-aware odds expected 26.7 (1980-81..2025-26); odds x1.86 (1.12 to 3.18); series odds x1.55 (1.24 to 1.93).

Does a smooth weight in usage rank and relative true shooting tie or beat the yes/no star-creator flag on hits and halve its rank jumps?

Title equity with the soft weight beats the yes/no flag on All-NBA (+1.1 points, +0.4 to +2.0) and top option on a playoff team (+1.3, +0.4 to +2.4); a 1-point shooting change moves no top-30 player 10+ places (flag: 34 of 570). Rule D: adopt.

Does a premium fitted on 1980-2008 forecast 2008-26 series better than the bracket odds alone?

The premium replicated (x1.56 fitted early, x1.64 refitted late) but the out-of-sample log-loss gain is not distinguishable from zero (-0.0055, -0.0195 to +0.0097). Rule C fails; the test passes only 33% of the time if the premium is real.

On held-out champions the gate does not beat the same flag without it (-0.051 to +0.006 log loss), so the declared rule renames the premium a lead-option (usage) premium. At series level the gate does help (-0.0064, -0.0119 to -0.0010).

Updated 2026-10-15: was +12.2% of slot squared error removed (slot + BPM) (+7.8% to +16.5% (90%, players)) before SiPM rerun 3 (2026-10-09); numbers refreshed from the current output.

Sample: 524 test players, 11 classes

SupportedHeld out+11.9% of slot squared error removed (slot + BPM) (+7.6% to +16.0% (90%, players))CP18

Markets, trades and cap 33

Can players and picks be priced in one currency (the asset engine)?

Yes, as a yardstick: one VORP costs about $14.4M on today's market; a #4 pick returns about $56M more than it costs. Projections tie a linear model 1-2 seasons ahead and beat it 3-4 ahead (+0.03 R²).

Sample: 2 seasons

SupportedHeld outone VORP costs about $14.4M on today's marketM06

Do engine prices predict what teams actually pay in trades?

No: on 69 trades, rank correlation −0.05 with picks paid, below the declared bar of 0.30. Engine dollars are an internal yardstick, not a trade-price forecast.

Sample: 69 trades

Not supportedClean nullon 69 trades, rank correlation −0.05 with picks paid, below the declared bar of 0.30M07

How much did 2026 trades of past All-Stars pay over the engine's price?

Five trades paid −$2M to $182M over the engine price ($23M to $179M with veteran contracts at zero); median premium about $14.2M.

Sample: 2026 trades

SupportedIn sample onlyFive trades paid −$2M to $182M over the engine price ($23M to $179M with veteran contracts at zero)M11

What is a draft pick worth by slot?

A #1 pick is worth about 2.8 tenth picks in value over eight seasons, and the chance of an All-NBA player falls faster than value does. Draft-class bootstrap intervals on the tab.

SupportedIn sample onlyA #1 pick is worth about 2.8 tenth picks in value over eight seasons, and the chance of an All-NBA player falls faster than value doesM01

Why is #1 worth more than the curve says?

#1 is worth about 1.50× the average of picks 2 and 3 (1.07 to 2.02); the #1 was his class's best in 8 of 28 drafts, more than any other slot (no other slot more than 3). How big the premium looks depends on the yardstick. Updated 2026-10-15: the count was 10 of 28 before the 2026-10-02 full re-run (BPM fix and 82-game minutes basis), which made Paul George (#10) the best of 2010 over John Wall and Kawhi Leonard (#15) the best of 2011 over Kyrie Irving.

SupportedIn sample only#1 is worth about 1.50× the average of picks 2 and 3 (1.07 to 2.02)M02

What do protections and swap rights do to a pick's value?

Top-4 protection removes about 31% of a mid-table pick's value, because it removes the rare hit. A swap right is worth more three drafts out than one (it behaves like an option).

SupportedIn sample onlyTop-4 protection removes about 31% of a mid-table pick's value, because it removes the rare hitM04

Do pick prices follow the title odds a player adds (convex) or his production (linear)?

Production, at least as well: on 69 pick-for-player trades since 2019, mean miss 3.89 vs 4.46 (difference +0.57, −0.09 to +1.28: cannot separate); 0 of 66 variants favor title odds.

Not supportedClean nullon 69 pick-for-player trades since 2019, mean miss 3.89 vs 4.46 (difference +0.57, −0.09 to +1.28: cannot separate)M12

Does the market misprice player types?

The obvious pattern (scorers overpaid, role players underpaid) is mostly a measurement artifact. Under both directions of the regression, 1 of 15 types is mispriced: big interior finishers, overpaid by $1.1-6.4M a season.

Not supportedClean nullUnder both directions of the regression, 1 of 15 types is mispricedM18

Do buyers pay more at the trade deadline?

For time, not talent: per game delivered before the next offseason, deadline buyers pay 1.65× the offseason price in picks (1.33 to 2.08); per unit of recent form about the same, 1.11× (0.89 to 1.40).

SupportedIn sample onlyper game delivered before the next offseason, deadline buyers pay 1.65× the offseason price in picks (1.33 to 2.08)M13

No consistent one: deadline buyers paid 1.41× (1.03 to 1.96) over 1999-2026, but only 0.88× (0.61 to 1.46) in 2019-26, and the trailing-form yardstick shows none.

Not supportedNot replicateddeadline buyers paid 1.41× (1.03 to 1.96)M15

Withdrawn. All six such trades were June trades counted as if the season were nearly over; priced as offseason trades they cost no more (0.86×, 0.46 to 1.82).

Ten more wins draw about 4.3% more fans a game that season (3.4 to 5.1) and 6.4% over three seasons, then the effect is gone. A two-season rebuild path goes with about 119,000 fewer Bulls tickets over five seasons (66,000 to 169,000).

In-game and strategy 17

Is clutch performance a trait?

Mostly no. A player's late-and-close edge carries into the next season at r 0.10 on court and 0.02 (−0.03 to +0.07) for players who change teams; his own shooting 0.07, and −0.003 under the top-10% leverage definition.

Not supportedClean nullA player's late-and-close edge carries into the next season at r 0.10 on court and 0.02 (−0.03 to +0.07)G03

Do players miss more free throws under pressure (the "unclenching" question)?

Yes: 1.8 fewer per 100 in the last two minutes of a close game than of a decided one, and 3.8 fewer when the throw can tie or take the lead in the last 30 seconds (1.7M throws, each player against himself).

SupportedIn sample only1.8 fewer per 100 in the last two minutes of a close game than of a decided one, and 3.8 fewer when the throw can tie or take the lead in the last 30 seconds ...G09

How much of a playoff game is three-point luck?

About 31% of what regular-season margins leave unexplained; the side with better three-point luck won 69% of games (e.g. the 2018 Rockets' Game 7).

SupportedIn sample onlyAbout 31% of what regular-season margins leave unexplainedB17

Can score, clock and possession give a calibrated win probability for every possession since 1996?

Yes: calibration slope 0.98 when each season is predicted from the others. Fit on 1996-2015 and tested on 2016-25, every model is too confident (0.88): leads are less safe since 2016.

SupportedHeld outcalibration slope 0.98 when each season is predicted from the othersG01

Who adds the most win probability, and does it repeat?

Gilgeous-Alexander led 2025-26. One season's total tracks the next (r 0.48 per possession); rank agreement with NBA.com's own leverage table 0.50.

SupportedHeld outGilgeous-Alexander led 2025-26G02

How much is transition worth?

17.2% of possessions, 0.16 points more per possession than the half court (2025-26). How often a team runs is stable (0.94); transition is 15.1% of the spread in team net ratings.

Sample: Team-seasons

SupportedIn sample only17.2% of possessions, 0.16 points more per possession than the half court (2025-26)G05

Is drawing fouls a skill?

As stable as free-throw shooting: year to year r 0.84 for free-throw rate and 0.86 for shooting fouls per shot, against 0.82 for FT%.

SupportedIn sample onlyyear to year r 0.84 for free-throw rate and 0.86 for shooting fouls per shot, against 0.82 for FT%G06

Do referee crews differ?

Yes: one crew calls about 1.2 more or fewer fouls per game than another (1 SD), and most of it carries over to the next season. Home-whistle differences (about 0.7 free throws a game) cannot be tested for persistence.

SupportedHeld outone crew calls about 1.2 more or fewer fouls per game than another (1 SD), and most of it carries over to the next seasonG08

Game context and schedule 47

Do players shoot worse right after they check in, compared with their own later shots in the same kind of stint?

A player's first shot after he enters is worse than his later shots, by 1.9 points per 100 shots. The deficit fades over about three minutes.

Sample: Every regular-season FGA 1996-97..2025-26 (5,933,661 shots, 2,839 players). Expected make rate from a league shot model (distance, shot type, clock, score, period) plus court location plus the shooter's own skill by shot class. Player-season fixed effects; player-cluster bootstrap. Replicates in each era alone: 1997-2009 -1.88 (90% interval -2.31 to -1.43); 2010-19 -2.22 (90% interval -2.70 to -1.75); 2020-26 -1.22 (90% interval -1.78 to -0.70).

SupportedReplicated out of sampleFirst FGA after a mid-quarter entry vs his 3rd+ FGA (within 8 min of entry): -1.91 (90% interval -2.20 to -1.64) points per 100 shots vs expected; -0.84 (90% interval -0.96 to -0.72) FG percentage points. By minutes since entry, relative to 3-5 min: 0-1 min -3.00 (90% interval -3.38 to -2.62), 1-2 min -1.25 (90% interval -1.60 to -0.94), 2-3 min -0.81 (90% interval -1.15 to -0.46). (90% interval -2.20 to -1.64)CS01

How do usage and efficiency change with time into a player's shift?

In the first two minutes of a shift, players take fewer shots and make fewer than expected. Their team also scores less. This holds for starters and bench players, every type and every era.

Sample: Every player-minute 1996-97..2025-26, cut into lineup-stint rows; within-player-season deviations; player bootstrap.
