FGA per 100 possessions: starters -1.12 (90% interval -1.18 to -1.06), bench -1.96 (90% interval -2.01 to -1.90). (90% interval -3.14 to -2.71)IG02

Is there a cold start in free throws, where shot selection cannot explain it?

With shot selection removed, the first free-throw trip after a player enters is about 2 percentage points worse. The longer he sat, the bigger the drop.

Sample: Every regular free throw 1996-97..2025-26 (technical, flagrant, take and clear-path FTs excluded), controls for position in the trip (1 of 2, 2 of 2 and so on), clutch and period. Eras alone: 1997-2009 -1.78 (90% interval -2.09 to -1.52); 2010-19 -2.01 (90% interval -2.33 to -1.71); 2020-26 -2.39 (90% interval -2.76 to -1.98).

SupportedReplicated out of sampleFirst regular FT trip of a mid-quarter stint vs later trips: -2.00 (90% interval -2.19 to -1.81) percentage points (player-season FE); player-game FE -1.62 (90% interval -1.83 to -1.41). Rest before entry 12+ min minus under 4 min: -1.19 (90% interval -1.57 to -0.78). (90% interval -2.19 to -1.81)CS04

Is the cold start caused by the dead-ball restart (a called play, a set defence) or by sitting on the bench?

The first shot after a timeout, by a player who stayed on the floor, is as good as his later shots. Bench time is what makes the first shot worse; a dead-ball restart alone does not.

Sample: Timeout restarts identified from possessions with a timeout before the first attempt (the timeout clock itself is not in every feed).

SupportedIn sample onlyFirst FGA after a timeout restart, players on court 3+ min: -0.15 (90% interval -0.53 to +0.22) points per 100 shots. (90% interval -0.53 to +0.22)CS05

Is the cold start specific to checking in mid-quarter, or does it follow any entry off the bench?

Bench players who enter at the start of a quarter start just as cold. Starters opening the second half do not start cold.

Sample: Same data and model as cold-start-1. Quarter-break entry = a player who sat at the end of one quarter and opened the next.

Not supportedClean nullFirst-FGA effect after a quarter-break entry -2.20 (90% interval -2.91 to -1.40); difference mid minus quarter-break +0.29 (90% interval -0.54 to +1.06). Game start -1.44 (90% interval -1.90 to -0.98); half start -0.04 (90% interval -0.51 to +0.37). (90% interval -2.91 to -1.40)CS03

Griffin's idea: shooters may start cold while post players score best from the post right after checking in. Does the data agree?

Spacers and post players show the same first-shot deficit. Post players' post-ups and rim shots right after entry are also below expected.

Sample: Player types per player-season (200+ FGA): post (top 15% in hook / short turnaround share), creator, spacer (top quarter in 3PA share), big (6-10+), other. Post-ups are a shot-label proxy, not play-type data.

Not supportedClean nullSpacer minus post first-FGA effect: -0.32 (90% interval -1.34 to +0.74). Post players' post-up and rim shots: -1.62 (90% interval -2.51 to -0.83). Spacers' threes: -2.63 (90% interval -3.81 to -1.39). (90% interval -1.34 to +0.74)CS07

How does shot-making vary with shot number in the game?

Descriptive: a player's first shot of the game is 1.23 points per 100 shots below his average (-1.44 to -1.02); shots 4-6 are +0.31. Players who start hot get more shots, so this table leans against flow.

Sample: 5.9M shots

SupportedIn sample onlyFirst shot of the game: -1.23 per 100 shots (-1.44 to -1.02)UE12

Griffin's hypothesis: catch-and-shoot role players start cold on threes at their first first-half entry, but not in the second half once they have played.

Low-usage shooters' first three after their first entry is well below expected. Their first three after re-entering in the second half is less cold, but the gap is not clear.

Sample: Catch-and-shoot role players = 1,790 player-seasons with usage below the median and 3PA share in the top third (examples: Joe Johnson 2004, Dorell Wright 2010, Quentin Richardson 2004, Peja Stojakovic 2007). Non-self-created threes; non-starter stints.

MixedIn sample onlyFirst three, first entry of the game in the first half: -4.36 (90% interval -6.27 to -2.50) points per 100 threes; first entry of the second half after playing in the first: -3.02 (90% interval -5.10 to -1.02); difference -1.34 (90% interval -3.97 to +1.33). (90% interval -6.27 to -2.50)IG01

Does shot-making fall with minutes into a stint (fatigue)?

Starter-level players shoot 2.34 points per 100 shots worse at 9+ minutes into a stint than at 3-6 minutes (-2.65 to -2.01). Bench players look better the longer they stay, but that is mostly coaches keeping hot players on.

Sample: 5.9M shots

SupportedIn sample onlyStarters, 9+ minus 3-6 minutes into the spell: -2.34 per 100 shots (-2.65 to -2.01)UE11

What do back-to-backs, travel and altitude cost?

A back-to-back costs 1.8 points of margin on the road and 1.5 at home; the cost has not shrunk over time.

SupportedIn sample onlyA back-to-back costs 1.8 points of margin on the road and 1.5 at homeG10a

How do players who play the second night of a back-to-back perform?

0.3 TS points worse and 0.17 game score per 36 lower; the drop does not grow with age, and those who play get slightly more minutes.

SupportedIn sample only0.3 TS points worse and 0.17 game score per 36 lowerG11

Beyond back-to-backs and three-in-four, does a crowded week cost?

Yes, but smaller than a back-to-back. Each extra game in the previous 7 days costs -0.34 (-0.50 to -0.21) points of margin, about a fifth of a back-to-back (-1.67), on top of the back-to-back, rest and three-in-four terms. It runs through defense (+0.40 (+0.24 to +0.52) points per 100 allowed). Four games in five nights: -0.39 (-0.82 to +0.07).

Sample: 68,414 team-games (34,207 games); ratings from games before each game

SupportedHeld outmargin per extra game in the previous 7 days: -0.340 (-0.501 to -0.213 (90%))GC12

Does regular-season rest pay off in the playoffs?

No design shows a payoff: playoff TS per 3 fewer minutes a game +0.04 (−0.11 to +0.22; effects under about 0.26 could not show). The one estimate clear of zero runs through injury: 10 points more games missed goes with 5.7 points less playoff availability.

Not supportedClean nullplayoff TS per 3 fewer minutes a game +0.04 (−0.11 to +0.22; effects under about 0.26 could not show)T20

Do teams score or defend worse when the game runs faster or slower than they are used to?

Not detectable. Per 3 possessions per 48 of mismatch (either direction), a team's own offense changes by +0.05 (-0.09 to +0.18) points per 100 against its pre-game expectation; both offenses together change by +0.11 (-0.18 to +0.37). Held-out years agree (+0.07 (-0.13 to +0.30)) and the next-opponent placebo is +0.11 (-0.02 to +0.22).

Sample: 68,414 team-games (34,207 games); ratings from games before each game

Not supportedClean nullown offense per 100, per 3 poss/48 of mismatch: +0.053 (-0.086 to +0.179 (90%))GC02

Does a team's result against its pre-game rating expectation change with the game's pace?

No. Against the pre-game gap the margin residual moves +0.18 (-0.04 to +0.45) points per 3 possessions per 48 (curvature -0.10 (-0.25 to +0.02)). By actual pace minus the team's norm, the average margin residual stays within about 0.1 points in every bin except the slowest (more than 6 below the norm: +0.32 (+0.07 to +0.55)).

Sample: 68,414 team-games (34,207 games); ratings from games before each game

Not supportedClean nullmargin residual per 3 poss/48 of gap: +0.185 (-0.043 to +0.448 (90%))GC03

Do half-court teams lose more than transition teams when the opponent plays faster?

The declared test is null: +0.10 (-0.11 to +0.34) points of margin per 3 possessions per 48 (half-court minus transition), but its placebos were not centred on zero. With own-pace controls (added afterwards) the contrast is -0.24 (-0.53 to +0.02), in the hypothesised direction but including zero; it is -0.57 (-1.01 to -0.18) in 2015-2025 and -0.13 (-0.45 to +0.18) in 1997-2014.

Sample: 68,414 team-games (34,207 games); ratings from games before each game

Not supportedNot replicatedhalf-court minus transition, margin per 3 poss/48 (revised spec): -0.238 (-0.530 to +0.021 (90%))GC04

Do teams built on defense hold up better than others when the opponent plays faster?

A small lead. Defense-first teams beat their rating expectation by +0.31 (+0.11 to +0.55) points of margin more than other teams per 3 possessions per 48 that the opponent is faster. The next-opponent placebo is -0.03 (-0.30 to +0.29); the held-out years give +0.43 (+0.05 to +0.87) and the early years +0.24 (-0.01 to +0.47).

What is playoff home court worth beyond the skill difference between the teams?

Within-series estimate 4.09 points (90% 3.61 to 4.57), 1996-2026; regular season 2.77 (2.56 to 2.96) by the same method; difference 1.32 (0.82 to 1.84).

Does the playoff home edge survive without fans and travel?

Supported by the 2020 bubble: same nominal hosts and 2-2-1-1-1 order, no fans or travel, home edge -0.07 (-2.58 to 2.45), 4.5 below nearby postseasons.

Does home court change the chance of winning the series, beyond skill?

Supported (model-implied): hosting is worth 6.6 percentage points against the opponent hosting (5.8 to 7.4), 8.0 for even teams. The direct series test (70 underdog-hosted series) gives 14.6 (5.5 to 23.6), consistent but noisy. Updated 2026-10-15: the model-implied value was 5.8 (5.1 to 6.5) before the 2026-10-09 fix that moved the mixed-model fit to its true optimum (series-skill spread omega 4.37 -> 1.70; agents/statsmodels-port/verify.json); a wider skill spread had been absorbing part of home court. The direct test did not move.

Sample: 435 series, 1692 always-played games; 34,487 regular-season games

SupportedIn sample only6.6 percentage points of series-win probability (vs opponent hosting); 3.0 vs neutral (5.8 to 7.4; direct test 5.5 to 23.6)PH07

What does one extra point of regular-season margin buy in seeding, home court, playoff games and title odds, by team?

Supported in simulation: one point of margin per game adds 0.25 home playoff games for the average team (0.31 hosted as series host) and 5.2 points of round-1 hosting odds; 0.42 home games for teams ranked 4th-8th; near zero for lottery teams and top seeds.

Sample: 10,000 simulated seasons per team and margin step

SupportedIn sample only+0.25 home playoff games, +0.31 as series host, +5.2 pts P(host round 1), +0.16 pts title odds per point (league average); 2.54 wins per point (real slope) (slope 90% 2.45 to 2.63; home games 0.24 to 0.25 with the playoff home edge at G12's 90% ends; Monte Carlo SE 0.01-0.03)CC03

How often were seeds, and series hosts, within one or two wins?

Mixed: adjacent seeds are often close (4th and 5th within 2 wins in 57% of conference-seasons; 6th and 7th within 1 win in 65%), but the host of a real best-of-7 series led its opponent by 2 wins or fewer in only 19% (median edge 7 wins).

Sample: 40 conference-seasons; 330 series

Yes: games 5-7 pooled carry 3.6 points beyond skill after correcting for which series reach them, -0.5 (-1.6 to 0.6) against games 1-4; the within-series design gives -0.5.

Is the home edge larger in game 7s and other deciding games?

Not distinguishable: the deciding-game term is -0.08 (-1.83 to 1.66), selection-bias corrected. Updated 2026-10-09 after the mixed-model fit was moved to its true optimum (it was +0.71 before). Hosts won 77 of 107 game 7s, about what home court plus skill predicts.

Does the side facing elimination outperform its series level?

Not supported, and the sign runs the other way: the side facing elimination plays about 1 point below its level (-0.97, 90% -1.81 to -0.12, selection-bias corrected). Corrected 2026-10-09: the earlier +0.08 (-0.79 to 0.95) came from a mixed-model fit that had stopped before its optimum (REML log-likelihood 16.4 lower). The numpy port (agents/statsmodels-port/verify.json) finds the true optimum (omega 4.37 -> 1.70). The fixed-effects version, corrected, agrees in sign (-1.37). One test among several in this family, so treat it as small and borderline.

Sample: 435 series, 1692 always-played games; 34,487 regular-season games

Does the result of game 1 change the host's game-2 margin beyond what skill predicts?

Suggestive: after a game-1 loss the host's game-2 margin beats the constant-skill model by +3.2 points (1.1 to 5.2); after a game-1 win it trails by -1.0. Both games are at the host, so venue cannot explain it.

Does running a play for the new player right away help or hurt?

First shots taken in the team's first possession after a player enters are much worse than first shots that come later.

Sample: Mid-quarter entries. Shot model already adjusts for shot type, distance, location and shot clock.

Do some players reliably start colder than others?

Players who start cold in even seasons are no more likely to start cold in odd seasons. Individual cold-start numbers are noise.

Sample: Players with 100+ mid-entry first FGAs in each half. Shrunk table covers 1352 players.

Who controls pace when two teams with different norms meet?

No one gets extra say. A game's pace is the league average plus each team's own pace deviation. The share of control is +0.49 (+0.48 to +0.50) for the faster team, +0.50 (+0.49 to +0.51) for the home team and +0.51 (+0.50 to +0.51) for the better team (0.5 = equal say).

Sample: 68,414 team-games (34,207 games); ratings from games before each game

Not supportedClean nullfaster team's share of control: +0.493 (+0.483 to +0.503 (90%))GC01

How do playoff games differ from the regular season?

Slower (−2.1 possessions per 48), lower-scoring (−3.5 per 100 raw; −1.3 beyond the better opponents) and more concentrated on the top two players (usage share 41% → 44%).

Do bigs or guards change most, against their own baseline, when the opponent plays faster?

Yes, slightly. Per 3 possessions per 48 of opponent pace, bigs (82 in and up) gain +0.26 (+0.17 to +0.35) points per 100 possessions and +0.38 (+0.24 to +0.53) TS percentage points relative to guards (75 in and under). Guards' own TS slope is -0.12 (-0.22 to -0.03); bigs' is +0.26 (+0.16 to +0.37).

No. Every group gains a little when the opponent plays faster, by about the same amount. Athletic (top third of 2015-16 fast-break speed for height) minus others: +0.11 (-0.04 to +0.25).

How does foul trouble change a player's game?

In foul trouble, players shoot less often and foul less, with no change in shot-making.

Is there a fatigue interaction between pace and back-to-backs or fourth quarters?

No. Being forced faster on the second night of a back-to-back changes the margin residual by -0.04 (-0.26 to +0.14) per 3 possessions per 48. In fourth quarters, a faster predicted game changes a team's own offense (Q4 minus Q1-Q3) by -0.17 (-0.37 to +0.04) per 100; the back-to-back interaction is +0.35 (+0.08 to +0.63), the opposite sign to fatigue.

Against their own norms, starters re-entering in the second half draw more free throws and run less in transition. Bench players' first stints are below their norm in shot-making.

The back-to-back cost grew because teams rest stars on the second night?

Only a small part. The second-night cost grew -0.92 points (-1.47 to -0.22) from 1997-2021 to 2021-26; the part running through top-two absences grew -0.11 (-0.21 to -0.01), about 12% of it.

Data quality and methods 8

How well is each input measured?

Counting stats are reliable at 500+ minutes (0.85 to 0.98). The noisy inputs are true shooting (0.70) and 3P% (half weight needs about 193 attempts); steals for size need about 316 minutes.

SupportedIn sample onlyCounting stats are reliable at 500+ minutes (0.85 to 0.98)E15

How wrong are listed heights?

The database stores one final height per player. Listings run +1.24 in over barefoot for players retired before 2019-20 and +0.61 in after.

SupportedIn sample onlyListings run +1.24 in over barefoot for players retired before 2019-20 and +0.61 in afterE16

Can a box-score stand-in measure athleticism?

No: the stand-in tracks 2015-16 in-game peak speed at rank correlation 0.09. Athleticism is unmeasurable from box scores before the combine era.

Not supportedClean nullthe stand-in tracks 2015-16 in-game peak speed at rank correlation 0.09E25

How much does survivorship distort aging curves?

Without a survivor correction, the 31-35 drop in 3P% is -0.47 league spreads; with it, -0.18. Shooters who keep playing are partly the lucky ones, and their next season regresses.

Sample: Player-season pairs, 200 player resamples

SupportedIn sample only3P% decline 31-35: -0.47 uncorrected vs -0.18 corrected (spreads) (-0.34 to -0.03 (corrected))TA08

Do shrinkage strengths fitted by prediction (empirical Bayes) beat hand-set ones?

Mostly close. Catch-and-shoot shrinkage was far too weak (k 49 → 215) and play-type efficiency badly under-shrunk (25 → 125-370).

SupportedHeld outCatch-and-shoot shrinkage was far too weak (k 49 → 215) and play-type efficiency badly under-shrunk (25 → 125-370)E12

Does weighting each pair by next season's minutes bias the survivor age curve?

Yes. The front-page delta weights each pair by the harmonic mean of both seasons' minutes; next season's minutes are an outcome. With equal weights the survivor curve peaks at 26 (26 to 28) and sits 0.18 spreads (0.14 to 0.23) lower at 28.

Sample: 10,827 at-risk pairs, 200 player bootstraps

SupportedIn sample onlyFront-page curve minus equal-weight survivor curve at 28: 0.18 spreads; at 33: 0.19 (0.14 to 0.23; 0.13 to 0.26 (90%))SV02

How do ABA values translate to the NBA scale?

ABA-to-NBA movers kept 0.76 of their ABA value spread (90% [0.608, 0.945]) with a level shift of -0.64 per 100 (90% [-1.04, -0.232]).

Sample: 104 ABA->NBA pairs, 1788 NBA pairs

SupportedIn sample onlyscale 0.76, shift -0.64 (scale [0.608, 0.945], shift [-1.04, -0.232])AG12

How strong was each era's league in absolute terms?

Untested: values here are relative to each season's league; an era-bridging model (players' value changes across league changes, with an age curve) could estimate absolute league strength.

Not supportedIn sample onlyAG15
Data through October 23, 2026. Ratings in points per 100 possessions; 0 is an average player. How the model works. By Griffin Hilly.
